People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Hi, thank you for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that at work, I’ll sit down to do something like an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really trying to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else. I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ll notice I missed the main point of what was being discussed.

chores, or duties in the workplace.\n  manifestation: If someone gives me three or four follow-up steps after a committee meeting, I may do the first one and maybe draft the second, but then the email, spreadsheet, or final submission sits unfinished until someone reminds me.\n- key: S006\n  name: Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance\n  description: Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to I mean, I try to be prepared and pay attention, but what finally made me come in was things like starting an EPIC quality report, or sitting in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later realizing my mind has wandered so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or I’ve missed the whole point of what was just discussed. It feels embarrassing to say, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or achieving your goals because of your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… maybe not in a dramatic way, but it does happen. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four follow-up items, I’ll do the first one, maybe start drafting the second, and then somehow the email or spreadsheet or final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me. I hate admitting that, because I really do intend to follow through. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / medical_or_health_services_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I mean, I can get a little tense if I realize I’ve missed something or I’m behind, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around anxious or on edge. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say hopeless. I mean, I have moments where I feel irritated with myself, but I still feel like things are basically okay and workable. Hopefully that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with remembering recent things. Maybe little normal things here and there, but nothing that stands out to me as, like, “I can’t remember what just happened.” Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… though honestly it shows up at work too. I mean, I try to be prepared and pay attention, but what finally made me come in was things like starting an EPIC quality report, or sitting in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later realizing my mind has wandered so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or I’ve missed the whole point of what was just discussed. It feels embarrassing to say, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or achieving your goals because of your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… maybe not in a dramatic way, but it does happen. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four follow-up items, I’ll do the first one, maybe start drafting the second, and then somehow the email or spreadsheet or final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me. I hate admitting that, because I really do intend to follow through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of feeling like I have to repeat something over and over. I mean, I can overthink things sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I have urges or thoughts that feel compulsive. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a wide range of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a wide range of things, no. I mean, I worry about normal life stuff, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I’m constantly worrying about everything all day. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue for me… is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ll notice I missed the main point of what was being discussed.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone sometimes, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down when your mind wanders like that?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. I mean, I get frustrated with myself sometimes, maybe a little embarrassed if I missed something, but I wouldn’t say I feel down when it happens. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I can get a little tense if I realize I’ve missed something or I’m behind, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around anxious or on edge. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say hopeless. I mean, I have moments where I feel irritated with myself, but I still feel like things are basically okay and workable. Hopefully that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with remembering recent things. Maybe little normal things here and there, but nothing that stands out to me as, like, “I can’t remember what just happened.” Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… though honestly it shows up at work too. I mean, I try to be prepared and pay attention, but what finally made me come in was things like starting an EPIC quality report, or sitting in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later realizing my mind has wandered so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or I’ve missed the whole point of what was just discussed. It feels embarrassing to say, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or achieving your goals because of your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… maybe not in a dramatic way, but it does happen. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four follow-up items, I’ll do the first one, maybe start drafting the second, and then somehow the email or spreadsheet or final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me. I hate admitting that, because I really do intend to follow through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of feeling like I have to repeat something over and over. I mean, I can overthink things sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I have urges or thoughts that feel compulsive. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a wide range of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a wide range of things, no. I mean, I worry about normal life stuff, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I’m constantly worrying about everything all day. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue for me… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are often preoccupied with worries about everyday things, even when there is no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can worry if there’s an actual problem or deadline, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting around preoccupied with worries when nothing is going on. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind wandering and trouble focusing are related to feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: I think stress can make it worse, sure, but… I don’t think it’s only when I’m overwhelmed. What finally made me come in is that I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has completely wandered. I’ll be rereading the same numbers, or I’ll realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m trying. It’s not that I don’t care. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable after my promotion… so about two years and four months ago. I had more responsibility, a bigger portfolio, and the systems I’d always used to keep myself on track just didn’t seem to hold up as well anymore.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart or anything, but that’s when the mind wandering and focus issues started feeling harder to manage. Does that answer it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with remembering recent things. I’ll be rereading the same numbers, or I’ll realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m trying. It’s not that I don’t care. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable after my promotion… so about two years and four months ago. I had more responsibility, a bigger portfolio, and the systems I’d always used to keep myself on track just didn’t seem to hold up as well anymore.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart or anything, but that’s when the mind wandering and focus issues started feeling harder to manage. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wandering and trouble focusing happen in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been obvious at work, I think. That’s where it really shows up because of the EPIC quality reports and the clinic metrics meetings. I’ll sit down fully meaning to follow along, and then a few minutes later I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nIn my personal life… I guess it may happen some, but it’s harder for me to separate out or notice. The work examples are what finally made me feel like, okay, maybe I should ask someone about this. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Maybe little normal things here and there, but nothing that stands out to me as, like, “I can’t remember what just happened.” Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… though honestly it shows up at work too. I mean, I try to be prepared and pay attention, but what finally made me come in was things like starting an EPIC quality report, or sitting in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later realizing my mind has wandered so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or I’ve missed the whole point of what was just discussed. It feels embarrassing to say, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or achieving your goals because of your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… maybe not in a dramatic way, but it does happen. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four follow-up items, I’ll do the first one, maybe start drafting the second, and then somehow the email or spreadsheet or final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me. I hate admitting that, because I really do intend to follow through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of feeling like I have to repeat something over and over. I mean, I can overthink things sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I have urges or thoughts that feel compulsive. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a wide range of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a wide range of things, no. I mean, I worry about normal life stuff, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I’m constantly worrying about everything all day. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue for me… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are often preoccupied with worries about everyday things, even when there is no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can worry if there’s an actual problem or deadline, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting around preoccupied with worries when nothing is going on. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind wandering and trouble focusing are related to feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: I think stress can make it worse, sure, but… I don’t think it’s only when I’m overwhelmed. What finally made me come in is that I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has completely wandered. I’ll be rereading the same numbers, or I’ll realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m trying. It’s not that I don’t care. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable after my promotion… so about two years and four months ago. I had more responsibility, a bigger portfolio, and the systems I’d always used to keep myself on track just didn’t seem to hold up as well anymore.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart or anything, but that’s when the mind wandering and focus issues started feeling harder to manage. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wandering and trouble focusing happen in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been obvious at work, I think. That’s where it really shows up because of the EPIC quality reports and the clinic metrics meetings. I’ll sit down fully meaning to follow along, and then a few minutes later I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nIn my personal life… I guess it may happen some, but it’s harder for me to separate out or notice. The work examples are what finally made me feel like, okay, maybe I should ask someone about this. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to your mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it has started to affect things outside of just me quietly struggling.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have affected clinic reporting deadlines, and I know that makes other teams wait on me, which I feel really bad about. And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "Determine if the patient's symptoms are better explained by generalized anxiety", "question": "Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?"}

What finally made me come in is that I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has completely wandered. I’ll be rereading the same numbers, or I’ll realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m trying. It’s not that I don’t care. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable after my promotion… so about two years and four months ago. I had more responsibility, a bigger portfolio, and the systems I’d always used to keep myself on track just didn’t seem to hold up as well anymore.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart or anything, but that’s when the mind wandering and focus issues started feeling harder to manage. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wandering and trouble focusing happen in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been obvious at work, I think. That’s where it really shows up because of the EPIC quality reports and the clinic metrics meetings. I’ll sit down fully meaning to follow along, and then a few minutes later I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nIn my personal life… I guess it may happen some, but it’s harder for me to separate out or notice. The work examples are what finally made me feel like, okay, maybe I should ask someone about this. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to your mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it has started to affect things outside of just me quietly struggling.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have affected clinic reporting deadlines, and I know that makes other teams wait on me, which I feel really bad about. And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do like things done correctly, especially when other people are depending on the work, but I don’t know that I’m constantly worried about perfection when things are going well.\n\nMaybe I can be a little hard on myself sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.8, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that at work, I’ll sit down to do something like an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really trying to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else. I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ll notice I missed the main point of what was being discussed.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone sometimes, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down when your mind wanders like that?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. I mean, I get frustrated with myself sometimes, maybe a little embarrassed if I missed something, but I wouldn’t say I feel down when it happens. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I can get a little tense if I realize I’ve missed something or I’m behind, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around anxious or on edge. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say hopeless. I mean, I have moments where I feel irritated with myself, but I still feel like things are basically okay and workable. Hopefully that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with remembering recent things. Maybe little normal things here and there, but nothing that stands out to me as, like, “I can’t remember what just happened.” Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… though honestly it shows up at work too. I mean, I try to be prepared and pay attention, but what finally made me come in was things like starting an EPIC quality report, or sitting in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later realizing my mind has wandered so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or I’ve missed the whole point of what was just discussed. It feels embarrassing to say, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or achieving your goals because of your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… maybe not in a dramatic way, but it does happen. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four follow-up items, I’ll do the first one, maybe start drafting the second, and then somehow the email or spreadsheet or final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me. I’ll sit down fully meaning to follow along, and then a few minutes later I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nIn my personal life… I guess it may happen some, but it’s harder for me to separate out or notice. The work examples are what finally made me feel like, okay, maybe I should ask someone about this. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to your mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it has started to affect things outside of just me quietly struggling.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have affected clinic reporting deadlines, and I know that makes other teams wait on me, which I feel really bad about. And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do like things done correctly, especially when other people are depending on the work, but I don’t know that I’m constantly worried about perfection when things are going well.\n\nMaybe I can be a little hard on myself sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or pick up a pen like anyone would, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially restless or like I can’t sit still when I’m trying to focus.\n\nFor me it’s more… I just lose the thread mentally. But physically fidgety? I don’t think that’s a big issue. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.8, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that at work, I’ll sit down to do something like an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really trying to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else. I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ll notice I missed the main point of what was being discussed.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone sometimes, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down when your mind wanders like that?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. I mean, I get frustrated with myself sometimes, maybe a little embarrassed if I missed something, but I wouldn’t say I feel down when it happens. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I can get a little tense if I realize I’ve missed something or I’m behind, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around anxious or on edge. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say hopeless. I mean, I have moments where I feel irritated with myself, but I still feel like things are basically okay and workable. Hopefully that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with remembering recent things. Maybe little normal things here and there, but nothing that stands out to me as, like, “I can’t remember what just happened.” Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… though honestly it shows up at work too. I mean, I try to be prepared and pay attention, but what finally made me come in was things like starting an EPIC quality report, or sitting in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later realizing my mind has wandered so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or I’ve missed the whole point of what was just discussed. It feels embarrassing to say, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or achieving your goals because of your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… maybe not in a dramatic way, but it does happen. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four follow-up items, I’ll do the first one, maybe start drafting the second, and then somehow the email or spreadsheet or final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me. I hate admitting that, because I really do intend to follow through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of feeling like I have to repeat something over and over. I mean, I can overthink things sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I have urges or thoughts that feel compulsive. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a wide range of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a wide range of things, no. I mean, I worry about normal life stuff, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I’m constantly worrying about everything all day. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue for me… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are often preoccupied with worries about everyday things, even when there is no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can worry if there’s an actual problem or deadline, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting around preoccupied with worries when nothing is going on. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind wandering and trouble focusing are related to feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: I think stress can make it worse, sure, but… I don’t think it’s only when I’m overwhelmed. What finally made me come in is that I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has completely wandered. I’ll be rereading the same numbers, or I’ll realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m trying. It’s not that I don’t care. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable after my promotion… so about two years and four months ago. I had more responsibility, a bigger portfolio, and the systems I’d always used to keep myself on track just didn’t seem to hold up as well anymore.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart or anything, but that’s when the mind wandering and focus issues started feeling harder to manage. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wandering and trouble focusing happen in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been obvious at work, I think. That’s where it really shows up because of the EPIC quality reports and the clinic metrics meetings. I’ll sit down fully meaning to follow along, and then a few minutes later I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nIn my personal life… I guess it may happen some, but it’s harder for me to separate out or notice. The work examples are what finally made me feel like, okay, maybe I should ask someone about this. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to your mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it has started to affect things outside of just me quietly struggling.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have affected clinic reporting deadlines, and I know that makes other teams wait on me, which I feel really bad about. And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do like things done correctly, especially when other people are depending on the work, but I don’t know that I’m constantly worried about perfection when things are going well.\n\nMaybe I can be a little hard on myself sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or pick up a pen like anyone would, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially restless or like I can’t sit still when I’m trying to focus.\n\nFor me it’s more… I just lose the thread mentally. But physically fidgety? I don’t think that’s a big issue. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced excessive worry about a specific situation or thing, such as your health, finances, or relationships, that you find difficult to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I worry about normal things sometimes, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing—health or money or relationships—that I’m excessively worried about or can’t control.\n\nAt least, nothing that stands out to me that way. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do like things done correctly, especially when other people are depending on the work, but I don’t know that I’m constantly worried about perfection when things are going well.\n\nMaybe I can be a little hard on myself sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or pick up a pen like anyone would, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially restless or like I can’t sit still when I’m trying to focus.\n\nFor me it’s more… I just lose the thread mentally. But physically fidgety? I don’t think that’s a big issue. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced excessive worry about a specific situation or thing, such as your health, finances, or relationships, that you find difficult to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I worry about normal things sometimes, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing—health or money or relationships—that I’m excessively worried about or can’t control.\n\nAt least, nothing that stands out to me that way. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.8, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that at work, I’ll sit down to do something like an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really trying to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else. I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ll notice I missed the main point of what was being discussed.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone sometimes, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down when your mind wanders like that?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. I mean, I get frustrated with myself sometimes, maybe a little embarrassed if I missed something, but I wouldn’t say I feel down when it happens. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I can get a little tense if I realize I’ve missed something or I’m behind, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around anxious or on edge. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say hopeless. I mean, I have moments where I feel irritated with myself, but I still feel like things are basically okay and workable. Hopefully that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with remembering recent things. Maybe little normal things here and there, but nothing that stands out to me as, like, “I can’t remember what just happened.” Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… though honestly it shows up at work too. I mean, I try to be prepared and pay attention, but what finally made me come in was things like starting an EPIC quality report, or sitting in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later realizing my mind has wandered so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or I’ve missed the whole point of what was just discussed. It feels embarrassing to say, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or achieving your goals because of your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… maybe not in a dramatic way, but it does happen. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four follow-up items, I’ll do the first one, maybe start drafting the second, and then somehow the email or spreadsheet or final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me. I hate admitting that, because I really do intend to follow through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of feeling like I have to repeat something over and over. I mean, I can overthink things sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I have urges or thoughts that feel compulsive. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a wide range of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a wide range of things, no. I mean, I worry about normal life stuff, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I’m constantly worrying about everything all day. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue for me… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are often preoccupied with worries about everyday things, even when there is no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can worry if there’s an actual problem or deadline, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting around preoccupied with worries when nothing is going on. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind wandering and trouble focusing are related to feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: I think stress can make it worse, sure, but… I don’t think it’s only when I’m overwhelmed. What finally made me come in is that I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has completely wandered. I’ll be rereading the same numbers, or I’ll realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m trying. It’s not that I don’t care. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable after my promotion… so about two years and four months ago. I had more responsibility, a bigger portfolio, and the systems I’d always used to keep myself on track just didn’t seem to hold up as well anymore.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart or anything, but that’s when the mind wandering and focus issues started feeling harder to manage. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wandering and trouble focusing happen in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been obvious at work, I think. That’s where it really shows up because of the EPIC quality reports and the clinic metrics meetings. I’ll sit down fully meaning to follow along, and then a few minutes later I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nIn my personal life… I guess it may happen some, but it’s harder for me to separate out or notice. The work examples are what finally made me feel like, okay, maybe I should ask someone about this. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to your mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it has started to affect things outside of just me quietly struggling.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have affected clinic reporting deadlines, and I know that makes other teams wait on me, which I feel really bad about. And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do like things done correctly, especially when other people are depending on the work, but I don’t know that I’m constantly worried about perfection when things are going well.\n\nMaybe I can be a little hard on myself sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or pick up a pen like anyone would, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially restless or like I can’t sit still when I’m trying to focus.\n\nFor me it’s more… I just lose the thread mentally. But physically fidgety? I don’t think that’s a big issue. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced excessive worry about a specific situation or thing, such as your health, finances, or relationships, that you find difficult to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I worry about normal things sometimes, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing—health or money or relationships—that I’m excessively worried about or can’t control.\n\nAt least, nothing that stands out to me that way. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced persistent and excessive worry about everyday things for more than six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I have day-to-day worries like anyone does, but I wouldn’t describe it as persistent or excessive, especially not for six months or more.\n\nIf anything, it comes and goes depending on what’s happening. I don’t feel like I’m worrying about everyday things all the time. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do like things done correctly, especially when other people are depending on the work, but I don’t know that I’m constantly worried about perfection when things are going well.\n\nMaybe I can be a little hard on myself sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or pick up a pen like anyone would, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially restless or like I can’t sit still when I’m trying to focus.\n\nFor me it’s more… I just lose the thread mentally. But physically fidgety? I don’t think that’s a big issue. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced excessive worry about a specific situation or thing, such as your health, finances, or relationships, that you find difficult to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I worry about normal things sometimes, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing—health or money or relationships—that I’m excessively worried about or can’t control.\n\nAt least, nothing that stands out to me that way. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced persistent and excessive worry about everyday things for more than six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I have day-to-day worries like anyone does, but I wouldn’t describe it as persistent or excessive, especially not for six months or more.\n\nIf anything, it comes and goes depending on what’s happening. I don’t feel like I’m worrying about everyday things all the time. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it feels separate from anxiety, mostly. I mean, I’m not sitting there worrying about something when it happens.

It’s more like… I’ll start an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later my mind has just drifted. Then I’m rereading the same numbers, or I realize I missed the point of what someone was saying.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.8, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that at work, I’ll sit down to do something like an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really trying to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else. I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ll notice I missed the main point of what was being discussed.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone sometimes, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down when your mind wanders like that?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. I mean, I get frustrated with myself sometimes, maybe a little embarrassed if I missed something, but I wouldn’t say I feel down when it happens. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I can get a little tense if I realize I’ve missed something or I’m behind, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around anxious or on edge. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about the future lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say hopeless. I mean, I have moments where I feel irritated with myself, but I still feel like things are basically okay and workable. Hopefully that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big problem with remembering recent things. Maybe little normal things here and there, but nothing that stands out to me as, like, “I can’t remember what just happened.” Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… though honestly it shows up at work too. I mean, I try to be prepared and pay attention, but what finally made me come in was things like starting an EPIC quality report, or sitting in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later realizing my mind has wandered so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or I’ve missed the whole point of what was just discussed. It feels embarrassing to say, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or achieving your goals because of your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… maybe not in a dramatic way, but it does happen. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four follow-up items, I’ll do the first one, maybe start drafting the second, and then somehow the email or spreadsheet or final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me. I hate admitting that, because I really do intend to follow through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of feeling like I have to repeat something over and over. I mean, I can overthink things sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I have urges or thoughts that feel compulsive. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a wide range of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a wide range of things, no. I mean, I worry about normal life stuff, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I’m constantly worrying about everything all day. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue for me… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are often preoccupied with worries about everyday things, even when there is no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can worry if there’s an actual problem or deadline, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting around preoccupied with worries when nothing is going on. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind wandering and trouble focusing are related to feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: I think stress can make it worse, sure, but… I don’t think it’s only when I’m overwhelmed. What finally made me come in is that I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has completely wandered. I’ll be rereading the same numbers, or I’ll realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m trying. It’s not that I don’t care. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable after my promotion… so about two years and four months ago. I had more responsibility, a bigger portfolio, and the systems I’d always used to keep myself on track just didn’t seem to hold up as well anymore.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart or anything, but that’s when the mind wandering and focus issues started feeling harder to manage. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wandering and trouble focusing happen in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been obvious at work, I think. That’s where it really shows up because of the EPIC quality reports and the clinic metrics meetings. I’ll sit down fully meaning to follow along, and then a few minutes later I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nIn my personal life… I guess it may happen some, but it’s harder for me to separate out or notice. The work examples are what finally made me feel like, okay, maybe I should ask someone about this. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to your mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it has started to affect things outside of just me quietly struggling.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have affected clinic reporting deadlines, and I know that makes other teams wait on me, which I feel really bad about. And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable after my promotion… so about two years and four months ago. I had more responsibility, a bigger portfolio, and the systems I’d always used to keep myself on track just didn’t seem to hold up as well anymore.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart or anything, but that’s when the mind wandering and focus issues started feeling harder to manage. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wandering and trouble focusing happen in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been obvious at work, I think. That’s where it really shows up because of the EPIC quality reports and the clinic metrics meetings. I’ll sit down fully meaning to follow along, and then a few minutes later I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nIn my personal life… I guess it may happen some, but it’s harder for me to separate out or notice. The work examples are what finally made me feel like, okay, maybe I should ask someone about this. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to your mind wandering and trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it has started to affect things outside of just me quietly struggling.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have affected clinic reporting deadlines, and I know that makes other teams wait on me, which I feel really bad about. And outside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is causing some real problems. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about making mistakes or not doing something perfectly, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do like things done correctly, especially when other people are depending on the work, but I don’t know that I’m constantly worried about perfection when things are going well.\n\nMaybe I can be a little hard on myself sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or pick up a pen like anyone would, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially restless or like I can’t sit still when I’m trying to focus.\n\nFor me it’s more… I just lose the thread mentally. But physically fidgety? I don’t think that’s a big issue. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced excessive worry about a specific situation or thing, such as your health, finances, or relationships, that you find difficult to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I worry about normal things sometimes, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing—health or money or relationships—that I’m excessively worried about or can’t control.\n\nAt least, nothing that stands out to me that way. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced persistent and excessive worry about everyday things for more than six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I have day-to-day worries like anyone does, but I wouldn’t describe it as persistent or excessive, especially not for six months or more.\n\nIf anything, it comes and goes depending on what’s happening. I don’t feel like I’m worrying about everyday things all the time. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wandering and trouble focusing are related to anxiety or are they a separate issue for you?\n\nPatient: I think it feels separate from anxiety, mostly. I mean, I’m not sitting there worrying about something when it happens.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll start an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting really intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later my mind has just drifted. Then I’m rereading the same numbers, or I realize I missed the point of what someone was saying.\n\nThat’s actually kind of what pushed me to come in, because it’s embarrassing and I don’t want it to affect my work. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
